**What exists now**

- cognitive-mcp already serves gRPC (`CognitiveToolService`, port 50052, bound to the WG interface IP) alongside HTTP/SSE on 3003 — `crates/op-cognitive-mcp/src/main.rs`. The transports are independently toggleable via the `cognitive_mcp` state plugin (`http_enabled` / `grpc_enabled` / `dbus_enabled`, or `COGNITIVE_MCP_HTTP_DISABLED` etc.), so you can turn HTTP/SSE off and leave gRPC + D-Bus up.
- Enabling an MCP server through the tree is the `mcp` state plugin: apply state with `servers.<name>.enabled = true` plus a transport.

**Three gaps before the CLIs can ride gRPC**

1. `CognitiveToolService` has no generic tool RPCs — it's all domain-specific (notebooks, sources, GeminiQuery…). The generic `ListTools`/`CallTool` surface only exists on D-Bus and HTTP. You'd add three RPCs to `cognitive.proto` mirroring the D-Bus interface.
2. Factory CLI, gemini CLI, and claude-code only speak MCP stdio or streamable-HTTP — none of them dial gRPC. So gRPC-as-transport means each CLI's config registers a **stdio shim**: a tiny binary the CLI spawns, which translates MCP JSON-RPC on stdin/stdout → gRPC calls to `10.200.0.1:50052` over the grpc-uplink. That keeps the identity anchor as the WG peer IP and means nothing HTTP-shaped is exposed. No such shim exists yet — `op-mcp-proxy` is LLM-proxy plumbing and `op-grpc-bridge` is domain services, so it'd be a new small bin (op-mcp has a server-side `transport/stdio.rs` you could reuse the framing from).
3. The `mcp` state plugin's transport field currently only models `stdio | sse | http` (`crates/op-plugins/src/state_plugins/mcp.rs:52`). Worth adding `grpc` (or treating the shim entry as `stdio` with the gRPC target as args — that works today with zero schema change).

So the enable flow becomes: tree state (`mcp` plugin) declares the server enabled with the shim command → each CLI's mcpServers config (`claude mcp add`, `~/.gemini/settings.json`, factory's `mcp.json`) points at that same stdio shim → shim dials gRPC over the uplink → HTTP/SSE transport disabled entirely.

Saved to memory. And yes — reflection changes the design for the better. Both pieces are already live on the 50052 listener in `crates/op-cognitive-mcp/src/server.rs`: the reflection service is registered and tonic-web-wrapped right alongside `CognitiveToolService`, so one WG-bound port serves native gRPC, gRPC-web over HTTP/1.1 (xraqy headers ride it through the ghostbridge interceptor), and full descriptor discovery.

That kills the gap I flagged earlier: **no generic `ListTools`/`GetToolSchema`/`CallTool` RPCs needed in `cognitive.proto`.** The shim does what grpcurl does instead:

1. On startup, hit reflection, pull the `FileDescriptorProto`s for `CognitiveToolService`.
2. Answer MCP `tools/list` by mapping each RPC to a tool — name from the method, input schema generated from the request message descriptor (proto3 → JSON Schema is mechanical).
3. Answer `tools/call` by building a `DynamicMessage` from the JSON args (`prost-reflect` does both directions) and invoking the method dynamically.

So adding an RPC to the proto automatically surfaces it as a tool in claude-code/gemini/factory with zero shim changes — the proto stays the single source of truth, which is the same property the D-Bus tree projection gives you internally.

The remaining work is just the shim bin (stdio MCP framing on one side — reusable from `op-mcp/src/transport/stdio.rs` — reflection + dynamic dispatch on the other, dialing `10.200.0.1:50052`) and registering it in each CLI's mcpServers config + the `mcp` state plugin entry in the tree. One thing to verify when building it: the ghostbridge interceptor either exempts `grpc.reflection.v1.ServerReflection` or the shim sends identity headers on the reflection stream too — otherwise discovery gets rejected before the first tool call.

Two violations in one: wrong path **and** a parallel name outside the projection.

The correct shape: xray lifecycle (start/stop/reload) is a **method on the existing `xray` plugin** (`op-plugins/src/state_plugins/xray.rs`, which already declares an `xray.run` tool), projected by the mirror at `/org/opdbus/v1/plugins/xray`. The standalone daemon's `request_name("opdbus.v1")` + `.at("/opdbus/v1/xray")` should be deleted, and `schema_bridge.rs:889`'s proxy retargeted to `org.opdbus.v1` / `/org/opdbus/v1/plugins/xray`. I already fixed the stray literal in `xray.rs:49` (`/opdbus/...` → `/org/opdbus/...`). The daemon refactor is a real change — I want your nod before I move start/stop into the plugin and delete the rogue name, since it changes how xray gets launched.

## 2. subid / sessionid + Gemma — recorded

So control-plane ops (adding peers, egress via dashboard) currently must go through the decoy. ## 4. subdomain resolution — concrete root cause
The hypervisor's `/etc/resolv.conf` is just `nameserver 1.1.1.1` — it **bypasses the local resolvers entirely**. You have `nextdns` on 127.0.0.1:53 and `dnsmasq` on 10.200.0.2:53 / 10.220.35.1:53, but nothing uses them, so every `*.ghostbridge.tech` resolves via public DNS to the **decoy** (129.153.134.63) — including `grpc.ghostbridge.tech`, which per your architecture should land on the hypervisor's grpc-bridge via xray, not the decoy. The fix is split-horizon: point the hypervisor at the local resolver and have xray (the subdomain forwarder) answer internal names to local/wg-xray addresses while the decoy stays a decoy.

The DNS change is one `resolv.conf` edit plus resolver records, but flipping it on the live router mid-migration can break resolution if the local resolver lacks the records — so I'd rather set up the records first and show you the diff.

**Two decisions for you:** (a) fold xray start/stop into the plugin + kill the rogue `opdbus.v1` name now? (b) netmaker server — relocate to hypervisor, or stays on decoy? With those answered I can execute the path fix and stage the DNS split-horizon.

## Unified model: Gemma is the routing brain

One Gemma instance in the xray stack, fed from the sled/schema state in SHM, produces three outputs that all flow into the dynamically-generated xray config:

| Input | Gemma decides | Output lands in |
|---|---|---|
| plugin/mutation | **subid** (`src/prj/sch/mut/obs/evt/exp` taxonomy) | OSCAL prop + config metadata |
| session/tag | **OpenFlow tag route** | OVS flows / xray routing rules |
| hostname | **subdomain → backend** | xray inbound/DNS rules (split-horizon) |

This collapses three static subsystems (dnsmasq records, nginx vhosts, hardcoded routing) into one generated artifact. The decoy (129.153.134.63) stays a pure decoy; Gemma answers internal `*.ghostbridge.tech` to local/wg-xray addresses. It also means the `resolv.conf` fix isn't a standalone edit — it's "point the host at the resolver Gemma drives," done as part of the same cutover.

## Where that leaves execution

The pieces now form one coherent build, in dependency order:

1. **Fix the path violation** — move xray start/stop into the `xray` plugin at `/org/opdbus/v1/plugins/xray`, delete the daemon's rogue `opdbus.v1` name, retarget `schema_bridge.rs`. (Also: get the mirror `org.opdbus.v1` actually running — it's down right now, only `org.opdbus.projection` is up.)
2. **Gemma generation hook** — sled mutation → zeroclaw plugin → Gemma → emits config.json carrying secrets + `sessionid` + `subid` + subdomain map.
3. **Cutover** — repoint xray at the generated config, point host DNS at the Gemma-driven resolver, retire the static records.

## DNS architecture (final)

**NextDNS resolves everything except domains we own**, and the NextDNS profile is **injected per-identity via the ghostbridge header** — so DNS is part of the same accountability loop as the rest of the stack, not a flat host resolver. That's already half-wired: `NEXTDNS_PROFILE_ID` flows into the generated xray config next to the footprint/trace headers in `schema_bridge.rs`. Base device profile is `689ec7`; NextDNS listens on `localhost:53` and `10.200.0.2:53` and resolves correctly (verified).

The resolution chain becomes:

```
client/host → split-horizon forwarder
                ├─ *.ghostbridge.tech (owned)  → Gemma/xray → internal / wg-xray addr   (never public, never decoy)
                └─ everything else             → NextDNS (127.0.0.1), per-identity profile from header → upstream
```

**The concrete bug:** the host's `/etc/resolv.conf` is `nameserver 1.1.1.1` (written Jun 8; the "injected via nextdns-srv" comment is stale — the s6 run script doesn't actually write it). So the host bypasses local NextDNS entirely *and* sends owned domains to public DNS → the decoy. Both wrong.

**Safe fix order** (I won't flip live until the carve-out exists, or owned domains would keep hitting the decoy):
1. Stand up the split-horizon forwarder rule — owned domains → local (Gemma/xray or dnsmasq `server=/ghostbridge.tech/<local>`), default → `127.0.0.1` (NextDNS). dnsmasq on `10.200.0.2:53` is the natural home for this.
2. Repoint `/etc/resolv.conf` at that forwarder, and find/fix whatever wrote `1.1.1.1` so it doesn't get clobbered back.
3. Owned-domain answers ultimately come from Gemma's generated map (same artifact as subid + tag routing).

## The mechanism xray uses

xray has native unix-domain-socket transport — `streamSettings.network: "ds"` with `dsSettings.path`. That's the primitive that makes nicless forwarding work. The generator already emits, per endpoint:

- **outbound** (`schema_bridge.rs:402`): `protocol: freedom`, `network: "ds"`, `dsSettings.path: "/run/<label>.sock"` — this is the leg that dials the container's socket. No IP, no port, no veth — xray just `connect()`s the unix socket.
- **routing rule** (`:422`): `inboundTag → outboundTag`, picking `to-<label>` for traffic that arrived on that endpoint.

So forwarding to a "nicless socket" is just: terminate the inbound, pick an outbound by routing rule, and that outbound is a `freedom`/`ds` dialer into the container's `.sock`. The container exposes only that socket (incus `proxy` device, `unix:` ↔ `unix:`), with no network interface at all.

## Two gaps for "everything is a unix socket" + subdomain routing

**1. The inbound side is still TCP.** Right now the generated inbounds (`:383`) are `dokodemo-door` on `127.0.0.1:<port>` — that's a loopback TCP port, not a socket. To be truly nicless on both sides, the inbound `listen` should be a domain socket too (`network: "ds"`, `dsSettings.path`), so the path is `socket → xray → socket` with zero TCP. The current form is `TCP-in → socket-out`, a transitional shape.

**2. Routing is by port/tag, not by subdomain.** The rules match `inboundTag` (which endpoint it came in on), not the Host header or SNI. For an actual **subdomain forwarder**, the routing rule needs `domain`/SNI matching → `outboundTag` → socket, e.g.:

```json

One ingress (REALITY/vless or HTTP), xray reads SNI/Host, the Gemma-generated rule maps it to the `to-<label>` freedom/ds outbound, which lands on the container's socket. No NIC anywhere in the chain.

The subdomain forwarder now forwards to nicless sockets the clean way — everything is a socket, no TCP middle-hop:

**Assistant:**

Now I can answer both precisely, grounded in the actual config. The key is the **incus `proxy` device**: it translates an in-container TCP port to a host-side unix socket, so the daemon keeps its normal ports but they live only on the container's loopback — never on a NIC.

## Mail

From `mail_server.rs`: the container runs normal Postfix/Dovecot binding the standard ports (587/465/143/993) — but on a **nicless** container, so those binds sit on loopback inside the namespace and are exposed to *nothing*. The plugin's `xray_socket_path: "/run/xray/mail-naive.sock"` is the bridge: an incus proxy device maps each in-container port to a host unix socket, and xray routes into it. So:

- **Client protocols (IMAPS 993, SMTPS 465, submission 587)** — these carry TLS with SNI, so xray routes `mail.ghostbridge.tech` by sniffed SNI into the mail socket, exactly like every other endpoint. No host port.
- **App access** — `op-grpc-bridge`'s `MailService` (`SendEmail`/`GetInbox`/…) gives fully port-free mail over the gRPC-on-socket path for internal clients.
- **The one real exception: inbound MX on :25.** Foreign MTAs open port 25 in *cleartext* — no SNI on the initial connect, so xray can't route it by name. That requires a genuine TCP:25 listener, and it lives at the **ingress boundary** (wg-xray / the decoy), terminating into the mail socket. The container still has no NIC; the port exists only at the edge.

So "no ports" holds everywhere except the public MX hop, which is irreducible because SMTP-25 has no SNI to route on.

## Netmaker

Netmaker splits the same way, and it's the cleaner illustration of *why* one port is irreducible:

- **Control plane (REST API 8081, MQTT/WSS broker)** — HTTP/WSS with SNI/TLS. Already socketized: the netmaker container has an `api-sock` proxy device (`connect: tcp:127.0.0.1:8081` ↔ `listen: unix:/run/netmaker/api.sock`) — same port→socket bridge as mail. `api.ghostbridge.tech` / `broker.ghostbridge.tech` route by SNI into that socket. No host port needed.
- **Data plane (WireGuard, UDP 51821)** — this is the irreducible one. WireGuard is connectionless UDP with no SNI, and more fundamentally **it's the substrate the whole socket fabric rides on**. The unix sockets, xray, the grpc-uplink — all of it travels *inside* WG tunnels. WG can't ride on top of itself, so its UDP port is real and necessary. That's not a violation of the model; it's the floor the model stands on.

So the honest rule across the stack: **everything above the WireGuard transport is socket + SNI, no ports.** The two places a real port must exist are (1) the WireGuard UDP listener that carries everything, and (2) the public SMTP-25 MX hop, because neither has an SNI to route on. Both sit at the edge; neither is on a container.

That also reframes your netmaker question from earlier — the server's control plane is already socket-ready via `api-sock`; what's keeping it down is the broker token timeout (broker on the decoy), not the networking model.

**Assistant:**

Two corrections to make — I had the WARP/fwmark relationship backwards, and let me define SNI properly.

## SNI (Server Name Indication)

SNI is a field in the **TLS ClientHello** — the very first message a client sends to open a TLS connection. It carries the hostname the client wants to reach (e.g. `qdrant.ghostbridge.tech`), and in classic TLS it's sent **in cleartext**, before any encryption is set up. Its original purpose is virtual hosting: one IP/port can serve many TLS sites because the server reads SNI to know which certificate/site to present.

For this stack, SNI is what makes **name-based routing into sockets** possible: xray peeks the ClientHello's `server_name`, matches it against the routing rules (the `domain: ["full:..."]` entries the generator now emits), and dials the corresponding `freedom`/`ds` outbound — all *before* terminating TLS. That's why mail (IMAPS/SMTPS), the grpc-bridge, and netmaker's API can ride one ingress into per-service sockets with no ports. The catch is exactly the edge cases we hit: protocols with **no SNI** on first contact (cleartext SMTP-25, raw WireGuard UDP) can't be routed this way. (Side note: ECH — Encrypted Client Hello — encrypts SNI so it's no longer visible on the wire; xray's REALITY relates to hiding it.)

## fwmark — I had it inverted

You're right, and it reverses what I wrote. The fwmark doesn't funnel WireGuard *into* WARP — it does the opposite: WG's own packets are marked so they **bypass** the WARP table (51820) and egress the real path, so the WG peer endpoint is the **origin client's real IP, not Cloudflare's**. That's the standard wg-quick behavior — without the mark, WG handshakes would get swallowed by WARP's default route and loop. So WARP carries *other* traffic; WireGuard goes direct, peer-to-peer, real-IP-to-real-IP. WARP is **not** the boundary the WG transport rides inside. Let me fix the note I just corrupted.

---

**Assistant:**

Corrected in memory. So the accurate layering is:

```
WireGuard  ← direct, peer-to-peer, real-IP↔real-IP; fwmark keeps it OFF WARP
   └─ unix sockets + xray SNI routing   ← east-west, no ports
        └─ nicless containers (incus proxy: in-ns port → host .sock)

WARP (wgcf)  ← separate egress for non-WG traffic, its own table 51820
```

WireGuard is the transport substrate and it reaches peers at their real IPs precisely *because* fwmark excludes it from WARP — Cloudflare never sees the WG path. WARP is a sibling egress for everything that isn't WG, not a layer the mesh sits inside.

And the honest "needs a real port / can't be SNI-routed" list shrinks to its true members: **inbound SMTP-25** (cleartext MX) and **WireGuard's UDP** itself — both because there's no TLS ClientHello (no SNI) to route on. Everything that speaks TLS-with-SNI — mail client protocols, netmaker's API socket, grpc-bridge, cognitive-mcp, the shim — rides one ingress into per-service sockets with no exposed port.

**User:**

the wgcf fwmark is pretty cool. it just works no configuration really and sits transparently

---

**Assistant:**

It really is — and the reason it's so transparent is that it's all happening one layer below where anything has to care about it. wg-quick reads the `FwMark` (and `Table`) directives from the wgcf.conf and, on bring-up, does three things atomically: marks the tunnel's own packets, installs an `ip rule` that says "anything carrying this mark, skip the WARP table," and populates table 51820 for everything else. So the policy split is pure kernel routing — no userspace proxy, no per-app config, no NAT hairpin. Packets just get sorted at the routing stage and neither side knows the other exists.

That's also why it composes so cleanly with the rest of the stack: WireGuard doesn't have to be "WARP-aware," and WARP doesn't have to be "WG-aware." The fwmark is the entire contract between them, and it's one integer. Your own WG mesh keeps real-IP endpoints, WARP quietly handles the default-route traffic, and nothing upstream — the sockets, xray, the containers — ever has to account for which tunnel it's on.

The one place it bites people is diagnostics, which is exactly what the memory note guards against: `ip route` shows `default via … dev eth0` and it *looks* like that's the egress, but the marked traffic never consults that route — it's resolved out of table 51820 first. So a "fix" to the eth0 default does nothing to WARP and vice versa. As long as you remember the mark is doing the steering, it stays the invisible, zero-touch layer it's supposed to be.

**Assistant:**

Right — and that's the part that ties the whole obfuscation story together, because the two directions hide WireGuard in completely different ways:

**Inbound — REALITY.** What an outside observer can actually reach and probe is the xray ingress, and that's the `ghostbridge-reality` inbound in the config (`dest: www.microsoft.com:443`, `serverNames: ["www.microsoft.com"]`). REALITY's trick is that it doesn't present a forged cert like an ordinary TLS proxy — it borrows the *real* TLS handshake of the target site. To a passive DPI box the connection is byte-for-byte a normal HTTPS session to www.microsoft.com, with a genuine, chain-validating Microsoft certificate. And if an active prober tries to poke it, the non-authenticated connection just gets proxied through to the actual microsoft.com — so the probe sees a real website, not a tell. There's no self-signed cert, no SNI that fails validation, no VPN handshake signature. "They just see the reality setting" is exactly it: the only observable fingerprint is *ordinary traffic to a major site*.

**Outbound — WARP.** General egress rides the wgcf tunnel, so to the destination world the traffic appears to originate from Cloudflare's WARP, not from your nodes and not as anything WireGuard-shaped.

**Underneath — direct WG.** And the WireGuard mesh itself isn't the surface anyone probes. It runs between your own trusted endpoints, kept off WARP by the fwmark so it's efficient and real-IP — but it's never what an untrusted observer is pointed at. The probe-able surface is REALITY (looks like Microsoft) and the egress is WARP (looks like Cloudflare). WireGuard as a recognizable protocol simply never appears on either.

So the layering does its job at the *observability* level, not just routing: REALITY removes the ingress fingerprint, WARP removes the egress origin, and the actual privacy transport sits in the blind spot between them. That's also why the decoy holds up — an observer scanning the decoy's public IP finds what looks like a real site over REALITY, with nothing that says "VPN" or "WireGuard" anywhere on the wire.

- **Unobservability / unblockability of the transport** — REALITY at the ingress makes it indistinguishable from ordinary HTTPS to a real site; the protocol has no VPN/WireGuard fingerprint.
- **Hard identity gating** — every connection carries the injected identity, tied to the real origin, and that header is the only thing that grants access.

what do i put for gemini settings.json for cognative-mcp

---

**Assistant:**

For Gemini CLI, `cognitive-mcp` is reached through the stdio→gRPC shim you built (`op-mcp-shim`) — Gemini speaks stdio MCP, the shim translates to gRPC+reflection against cognitive-mcp on `:50052`. So `settings.json` points at the shim binary, not at the gRPC endpoint directly.

Put this in `~/.gemini/settings.json`:

```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "cognitive-mcp": {
      "command": "/usr/local/bin/op-mcp-shim",
      "args": ["--endpoint", "http://10.200.0.1:50052"],
      "env": {
        "GHOSTBRIDGE_FOOTPRINT": "gemini-cli",
        "GHOSTBRIDGE_TRACE_ID": "gemini-session-1"
      }
    }
  }
}
```

Notes tied to how the shim actually works:

- **`command`** is the shim, because Gemini launches an MCP server as a child process and talks JSON-RPC over its stdin/stdout. The shim is what does `initialize` / `tools/list` / `tools/call` and dispatches each tool as a unary gRPC call via `DynamicCodec`.
- **`--endpoint`** defaults to `http://10.200.0.1:50052` inside the shim already, so you can drop the `args` line entirely if that's your target. Set it explicitly only if cognitive-mcp is somewhere else.
- **`env` headers** are what get injected as `x-ghostbridge-footprint` / `x-ghostbridge-trace-id` gRPC metadata. The ghostbridge interceptor only checks header *presence*, so any non-empty values pass the gate — but set them to something identifying so the sled etches a meaningful peer.
- Tools show up in Gemini named `<service>_<method>` (e.g. `Cognitive_GetHealth`), matching what we verified (18 tools, GetHealth → healthy).

Two things worth confirming before it'll connect:

1. **Is the shim reachable on that endpoint from where Gemini runs?** If Gemini is on the laptop and `10.200.0.1` is the WG gateway, the laptop needs the wg-uplink up. If you're "everything is a socket" here too, point `--endpoint` at a `unix:` target instead — say the word and I'll check whether the shim's tonic channel builder accepts a UDS path or needs a small patch.

2. **`trust`/`timeout`** — Gemini optionally takes `"trust": true` to skip the per-tool confirmation prompt, and `"timeout": 30000` if reflection + first call is slow. Want me to add those?

## The Concert Analogy

> You arrive at a venue. You hand your keys to the **valet** (WireGuard). The valet parks your car and gives you a **claim ticket** (session ID). At the door, you exchange the ticket for an **all-access wristband** (the Ghostbridge identity sled). Every backstage door checks the wristband — not your car, not your ticket — just the wristband. Your car (the WireGuard tunnel) never enters the venue.

## Stage 1 — The Valet: WireGuard Handshake & Session ID Derivation

**Files:** `crates/op-gateway/src/wireguard_auth.rs:529-633`

When a peer connects over WireGuard, the gateway derives three cryptographic artifacts:

### Session ID (the claim ticket)
```rust
// wireguard_auth.rs:529-540
let input = format!("WG-SESSION-{}-{}", peer_pubkey, Self::current_timestamp());
let session_ids = self.crypto_engine.generate_session_ids_batch(&[input.as_bytes()]);
// → BLAKE2s hash → 128-bit hex string
```
- Input: Curve25519 peer public key + current timestamp
- Algorithm: **SIMD-accelerated BLAKE2s**
- Output: 16-byte hex session ID — the peer's claim ticket

### PSK (stable parking spot number)
```rust
// wireguard_auth.rs:542-593
let salt = b"WG-STABLE-PSK-2024";  // fixed — no timestamp
// Argon2 KDF over peer pubkey
```
Deliberately **does not rotate** — rotating it would lock peers out mid-session, like changing the valet lot number while cars are still parked.

### Session Key (per-visit ephemeral)
```rust
// wireguard_auth.rs:595-633
let server_nonce = self.generate_server_nonce().await?;
// Argon2 + nonce → ephemeral key, rotated per connection
```

### The Session Record (the claim stub kept by the valet)
```rust
// wireguard_auth.rs:200-215
pub struct WireGuardSession {
    pub session_id: String,        // BLAKE2s(pubkey + timestamp)
    pub peer_pubkey: String,
    pub psk: String,               // stable Argon2
    pub expires_at: u64,           // 1 hour TTL
    pub auth_method: String,       // "wireguard"
    pub key_rotation_count: u32,
    // ...
}
```
Persisted to SQLite (`wireguard_sessions` table) and cached in `DashMap` (no SQL in the hot path).

---

## Stage 2 — The Wristband Printer: IdentitySled

**Files:** `crates/op-mcp-proxy/src/sled.rs`, `crates/op-identity/src/schema_bridge.rs`

Once the WireGuard session is established, a **152-byte shared-memory struct** (`/dev/shm/plugin_schema.dat`) is stamped — the all-access wristband:

```
IdentitySled layout (repr(C), 152 bytes):
  [ 0.. 32]  wireguard_pubkey    — who you are
  [32.. 40]  mutation_index      — which version of the schema you authenticated against
  [40.. 72]  hashed_footprint    — Blake3(current schema state) — the hologram on the wristband
  [72.. 88]  trace_id            — UUID v4 — your unique wristband serial number
  [88.. 92]  schema_version
  [92..152]  reserved
  [192..208] nextdns_profile     — e.g., "689ec7" — your VIP section access
```

The **footprint** (Blake3 hash of the live schema state) is the hologram — it proves the wristband was issued by tonight's venue, not a forgery from last week's show.

The **trace_id** is the serial number stamped on your wrist — immutable, linked to a Qdrant vector record for full accountability.

### NextDNS Profile (the VIP section pass)
```python
# deploy/nextdns/upsert-rewrite.py
# Profile ID: 689ec7
# Upserts DNS rewrites: dashboard.3tched.com → 10.0.0.1
```
The NextDNS profile ID travels with the identity sled. It tells the DNS layer which routing rules apply to this peer — their section of the venue.

## Stage 3 — The Door Scanner: xray Header Injection

**File:** `deploy/incus/privacy-xray-ingress/etc/xray/config.json:47-50`

xray sits at the venue entrance. It reads the wristband (sled) and stamps the metadata onto every gRPC call:

```json
"grpcSettings": {
  "serviceName": "Ghostbridge.StateSync",
  "multiMode": true,
  "metadata": {
    "X-Ghostbridge-Footprint": "<Blake3 footprint hex>",
    "X-Ghostbridge-Trace-ID":  "<UUID v4 hex>"
  }
}
```

At this point, **WireGuard is dropped**. The tunnel delivered the peer to the door; the tunnel itself does not go backstage. xray strips the WireGuard framing and forwards a plain gRPC call carrying only the two header stamps. The car stays in the lot.

The NextDNS profile influences DNS resolution at this layer — `selector_ip` routing directs packets to the correct Incus container before they ever reach the gRPC endpoint.

## Stage 4 — The Backstage Guard: GhostbridgeInterceptor

**File:** `crates/op-grpc-bridge/src/interceptor.rs:34-84`

Every gRPC call hits this Tonic middleware on port **18789** before any handler sees it:

```rust
pub fn ghostbridge_interceptor(mut req: Request<()>) -> Result<Request<()>, Status> {
    // 1. Wristband must be present
    let footprint_value = req.metadata().get("x-ghostbridge-footprint").cloned();
    let trace_value    = req.metadata().get("x-ghostbridge-trace-id").cloned();

    if footprint_value.is_none() || trace_value.is_none() {
        return Err(Status::unauthenticated(
            "A.N.N.A. Scribe: Missing Ghostbridge Identity Sled. Connection Dropped.",
        ));
    }

    // 2. Zero-copy mmap read of the live sled (no DB hit, no network hop)
    let (sled_ptr, _mmap) = op_identity::read_sled()?;
    let sled = unsafe { &*sled_ptr };

    // 3. Hologram check — footprint must match tonight's schema state exactly
    let expected_footprint = hex::encode(sled.hashed_footprint);
    if request_footprint != expected_footprint {
        return Err(Status::permission_denied(
            "A.N.N.A. Scribe: Temporal Hash Mismatch. Session footprint out of sync.",
        ));
    }

    // 4. Embed trace ID into gRPC extensions for downstream accountability
    req.extensions_mut().insert(trace_val);
    Ok(req)
}
```

**Validity rule:** the sled is live only if `hashed_footprint != [0u8;32]` AND `trace_id != [0u8;16]`. A zero field means the wristband was never properly stamped — instant ejection.

The check is **zero-copy** — the sled lives in `tmpfs` (`/dev/shm`), mmap'd directly into the process. No SQL, no network, no disk. The guard glances at your wrist under UV light in milliseconds.

---

## Stage 5 — Backstage: gRPC Routing to op-dbus

**File:** `crates/op-mcp-proxy/src/main.rs:94-105`

Once the interceptor passes the call, it routes to the internal gRPC service:

```
Default: http://10.200.0.2:50051  (op-dbus, inside the privacy network)
```

Subnet assignments per peer come from `schemas/plugin/privacy_routes.json`:

```json
{
  "wireguard_public_key": "P8c9Kjnv4B3r6C4+...",
  "assigned_ip":  "10.100.0.2/32",    // WireGuard tunnel allocation
  "selector_ip":  "10.100.0.2",       // OpenFlow packet-match label
  "container_name": "privacy-route-550e8400"
}
```

OpenFlow matches `selector_ip` and routes to the correct Incus container. The WireGuard subnet (`10.100.0.0/24`) exists only to give each peer a unique identity label — it is never forwarded as a WireGuard packet beyond the gateway.

A Linux user session (`wg-{first12ofpubkey}`) is created via `loginctl enable-linger`, giving the peer a D-Bus session and `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` — the backstage room with their name on the door.

## Complete Flow, Annotated

```
[Peer]
  │  WireGuard UDP (Curve25519 handshake)
  ▼
[op-gateway / WireGuard endpoint]
  │  BLAKE2s(pubkey + timestamp) → session_id        ← claim ticket issued
  │  Argon2(pubkey, stable-salt) → PSK               ← stable parking spot
  │  Argon2(pubkey, nonce)       → session_key        ← tonight's coat check
  │  Write IdentitySled → /dev/shm/plugin_schema.dat  ← wristband stamped
  │
  │  WireGuard framing DROPPED here ──────────────────← car stays with valet
  ▼
[xray ingress]
  │  Reads sled: footprint + trace_id + nextdns_profile
  │  Injects gRPC metadata headers:
  │    X-Ghostbridge-Footprint: <blake3-hex>
  │    X-Ghostbridge-Trace-ID:  <uuid4-hex>
  │  NextDNS profile → DNS rewrites applied (VIP section routing)
  ▼
[GhostbridgeInterceptor — port 18789]
  │  Header present?    No  → 401 "Connection Dropped"
  │  Footprint matches? No  → 403 "Temporal Hash Mismatch"
  │  Zero-copy mmap sled read (tmpfs, no DB)
  │  Inject trace_id into gRPC extensions
  ▼
[op-dbus gRPC — 10.200.0.2:50051]
  │  Full access to internal services
  │  Trace ID rides every call → Qdrant vector linkage for audit
  ▼
[Incus container — privacy-route-{user_id}]
  └─ Linux user session, D-Bus activated, $XDG_RUNTIME_DIR alive
```

---

## Why This Is Genuinely Zero-Trust

| Zero-Trust Principle | Implementation |
|---|---|
| Never trust, always verify | Every gRPC call re-validates the live sled footprint — no cached "logged in" state |
| Verify cryptographic state, not identity claims | Footprint is Blake3 of current schema — a stale wristband from an old schema is invalid |
| Least privilege | NextDNS profile scopes DNS; `assigned_ip/32` scopes the subnet to exactly one peer |
| Full audit trail | Trace ID links every call to a Qdrant vector record — immutable, append-only |
| No implicit trust from network location | Being inside `10.100.0.0/24` grants nothing — the wristband check happens regardless |

The WireGuard subnet is purely a **cryptographic identity label** — it gets the peer through the front door and into the valet system. The moment they're inside, the car (the tunnel) stays parked. Everything backstage runs on the wristband alone.

# The Zero-Trust Identity Pipeline

## The Concert Analogy

> You arrive at the venue and hand your keys to the **valet** (the WireGuard gateway). The valet parks your car — *and the car never moves again* — then hands you a **claim ticket** (session ID derived from your key). At the door you trade the ticket for an **all-access wristband** (the Ghostbridge identity sled, stamped into shared memory). From that point on, every backstage door checks **only the wristband under UV light** — not your car, not your ticket, not which entrance you came through. The car (your WireGuard tunnel) stays in the lot. Nothing you drove in on goes backstage.

The load-bearing idea: **the tunnel is an authentication event, not a transport path.** WireGuard's job is to *prove who you are once* and deposit a tamper-evident token; it is explicitly *not* a route into the interior. This is why a peer on `10.0.0.0/x` cannot address `10.200.0.1` — there is no road from the parking lot to the stage, by design.

---

## Stage 1 — The Valet: Handshake, Session Derivation, and Tunnel Termination
**`crates/op-gateway/src/wireguard_auth.rs:529-633`**

A peer completes a Curve25519 WireGuard handshake. The gateway derives three artifacts, each with a deliberately different rotation policy:

**Session ID — the claim ticket (rotates per connection)**
```rust
let input = format!("WG-SESSION-{}-{}", peer_pubkey, Self::current_timestamp());
let session_ids = self.crypto_engine.generate_session_ids_batch(&[input.as_bytes()]);
// BLAKE2s(pubkey ‖ timestamp) → 128-bit hex
```
Timestamp-salted, so it's unique per visit — a fresh stub every time you valet.

**PSK — the parking-spot number (deliberately stable)**
```rust
let salt = b"WG-STABLE-PSK-2024";   // fixed: no timestamp
// Argon2(pubkey, stable_salt) → PSK
```
It must *not* rotate: re-deriving it mid-session would renegotiate the tunnel and eject live peers — like renumbering the lot while cars are still parked.

**Session key — tonight's coat check (ephemeral, per-connection nonce)**
```rust
let server_nonce = self.generate_server_nonce().await?;
// Argon2(pubkey, server_nonce) → ephemeral session key
```

**The session record** (cached in `DashMap`, persisted to the `wireguard_sessions` SQLite table — SQL stays off the hot path):
```rust
pub struct WireGuardSession {
    pub session_id: String,   // BLAKE2s(pubkey ‖ timestamp)
    pub peer_pubkey: String,
    pub psk: String,          // stable Argon2
    pub expires_at: u64,      // 1-hour TTL
    pub auth_method: String,  // "wireguard"
    pub key_rotation_count: u32,
}
```

> **⚠ This is where the car is parked.** The WireGuard transport terminates *here*, at the gateway. Everything downstream is plain gRPC carrying header stamps — no WG framing survives past this boundary. The peer's `10.0.0.x` address has done its only job (prove identity) and is now just a label.

---

## Stage 2 — The Wristband Printer: IdentitySled
**`crates/op-mcp-proxy/src/sled.rs`, `crates/op-identity/src/schema_bridge.rs`**

With the session established, a `#[repr(C)]` struct is stamped into `tmpfs` at `/dev/shm/plugin_schema.dat` — the all-access wristband, readable by any in-venue process via zero-copy mmap:

```
IdentitySled  (repr(C))           ⚠ reconcile 152 vs 208 — see note above
  [  0.. 32]  wireguard_pubkey   — who you are (Curve25519, 32B)
  [ 32.. 40]  mutation_index     — which schema generation you authenticated against
  [ 40.. 72]  hashed_footprint   — Blake3(live schema state) — the hologram
  [ 72.. 88]  trace_id           — UUID v4 — wristband serial number
  [ 88.. 92]  schema_version
  [ 92..192]  reserved
  [192..208]  nextdns_profile    — e.g. "689ec7" — your VIP-section pass
```

- **`hashed_footprint`** is the hologram: a Blake3 digest of the *live* schema state at issue time. A wristband from last week's show hashes to a different value and is rejected — you can't reuse a stale identity against a mutated schema. This is the "verify cryptographic state, not a stored claim" property.
- **`trace_id`** is the serial number — immutable, and linked downstream to a Qdrant vector record so every action is attributable.
- **`nextdns_profile`** travels *with* the wristband, coupling DNS resolution to identity (which "section" of the venue your queries resolve into). Profile `689ec7` (Ghostbridge-Incus) is the base.

> ⚠ **Validity is liveness, not signature.** A sled is valid iff `hashed_footprint != [0u8;32]` **and** `trace_id != [0u8;16]`. The security claim is "the bearer can read the current live sled," not "the bearer signed a challenge." That's a coupling to fresh shared state — strong against replay of stale identities, and it's what you actually built. Worth stating plainly rather than implying PKI.

---

## Stage 3 — The Door Scanner: xray Header Injection
**`deploy/incus/privacy-xray-ingress/etc/xray/config.json:47-50`**

xray is the entrance. By the time a call reaches it, **WireGuard is already gone** (parked back at Stage 1). xray reads the live sled and stamps its two fields onto every gRPC call as metadata:

```json
"grpcSettings": {
  "serviceName": "Ghostbridge.StateSync",
  "multiMode": true,
  "metadata": {
    "X-Ghostbridge-Footprint": "<blake3 footprint hex>",
    "X-Ghostbridge-Trace-ID":  "<uuid v4 hex>"
  }
}
```

Externally this ingress is a **REALITY** front — to any observer it is indistinguishable from ordinary HTTPS to a real site, so there's no VPN fingerprint to block. The `nextdns_profile` carried on the sled selects the DNS rewrite set that steers `selector_ip` routing to the correct Incus container *before* the call reaches a gRPC endpoint.

---

## Stage 4 — The Backstage Guard: GhostbridgeInterceptor
**`crates/op-grpc-bridge/src/interceptor.rs:34-84` — Tonic middleware, port 18789**

Every call is re-checked here before any handler runs. No "logged-in" state is trusted; the wristband is re-read live on *each* call:

```rust
pub fn ghostbridge_interceptor(mut req: Request<()>) -> Result<Request<()>, Status> {
    // 1. Wristband present?
    let footprint_value = req.metadata().get("x-ghostbridge-footprint").cloned();
    let trace_value     = req.metadata().get("x-ghostbridge-trace-id").cloned();
    if footprint_value.is_none() || trace_value.is_none() {
        return Err(Status::unauthenticated(
            "A.N.N.A. Scribe: Missing Ghostbridge Identity Sled. Connection Dropped.",
        ));
    }

    // 2. Zero-copy mmap of the live sled — no DB, no network, no disk
    let (sled_ptr, _mmap) = op_identity::read_sled()?;
    let sled = unsafe { &*sled_ptr };

    // 3. Hologram check — footprint must equal tonight's live schema hash
    let expected_footprint = hex::encode(sled.hashed_footprint);
    if request_footprint != expected_footprint {
        return Err(Status::permission_denied(
            "A.N.N.A. Scribe: Temporal Hash Mismatch. Session footprint out of sync.",
        ));
    }

    // 4. Carry trace_id forward for downstream accountability
    req.extensions_mut().insert(trace_val);
    Ok(req)
}
```

The check is **zero-copy** against `tmpfs` — the guard glances at your wrist under UV in microseconds. No SQL hit, no network hop, nothing cached that could go stale between the door and the stage. A `Temporal Hash Mismatch` means the schema mutated out from under a wristband issued against an older generation — instant ejection.

---

## Stage 5 — Backstage: Routing to op-dbus
**`crates/op-mcp-proxy/src/main.rs:94-105`**

Past the guard, the call routes to the internal service on the grpc-uplink plane:

```
Default: http://10.200.0.2:50051   (op-dbus, inside the privacy network)
```

Per-peer placement comes from `schemas/plugin/privacy_routes.json`:
```json
{
  "wireguard_public_key": "P8c9Kjnv4B3r6C4+...",
  "assigned_ip":  "10.100.0.2/32",   // tunnel allocation — an identity LABEL
  "selector_ip":  "10.100.0.2",      // OpenFlow match key
  "container_name": "privacy-route-550e8400"
}
```

> The `10.100.0.0/24` subnet is **never forwarded as WireGuard** beyond the gateway. It exists purely to give each peer a unique L3 *label* that OpenFlow can match (`selector_ip`) to steer packets to the right Incus container. It is identity expressed as an address, not a routable path. (This is the same reason `10.200` is unreachable from a peer: these subnets are *labels and interior planes*, not a contiguous routed network.)

A Linux user session `wg-{first12_of_pubkey}` is created via `loginctl enable-linger`, giving the peer a D-Bus session bus and `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` — the dressing room with their name on the door.

## Complete Flow

```
[Peer]
  │  WireGuard UDP — Curve25519 handshake
  ▼
[op-gateway]  ── the valet
  │  BLAKE2s(pubkey ‖ timestamp) → session_id     claim ticket (per-visit)
  │  Argon2(pubkey, stable_salt) → PSK            parking spot  (stable)
  │  Argon2(pubkey, nonce)       → session_key    coat check    (ephemeral)
  │  stamp IdentitySled → /dev/shm/plugin_schema.dat   wristband printed
  │
  │  ══ WireGuard TERMINATES HERE ══              car stays in the lot
  ▼
[xray ingress]  ── the door (REALITY: looks like plain HTTPS)
  │  read sled → inject gRPC metadata:
  │    X-Ghostbridge-Footprint: <blake3 hex>
  │    X-Ghostbridge-Trace-ID:  <uuid4 hex>
  │  nextdns_profile → DNS rewrites → selector_ip routing
  ▼
[GhostbridgeInterceptor : 18789]  ── the backstage guard
  │  headers present?   no → 401  "Connection Dropped"
  │  footprint == live sled?  no → 403  "Temporal Hash Mismatch"
  │  zero-copy mmap read (tmpfs)   — re-verified on EVERY call
  │  inject trace_id → gRPC extensions
  ▼
[op-dbus gRPC : 10.200.0.2:50051]
  │  trace_id rides every call → Qdrant vector linkage (audit)
  ▼
[Incus container : privacy-route-{user}]
  └─ Linux user session, D-Bus bus + $XDG_RUNTIME_DIR live
```

---

## Why This Is Genuinely Zero-Trust

| Principle | Implementation |
|---|---|
| Never trust, always verify | Live sled re-read and footprint re-checked on *every* gRPC call — no cached session state |
| Verify cryptographic *state*, not a claim | Footprint = Blake3 of the current schema; a wristband from an older generation fails the hologram check |
| Least privilege | `nextdns_profile` scopes DNS; `assigned_ip/32` scopes the peer to exactly one address-as-label |
| Full audit trail | `trace_id` links every call to an append-only Qdrant vector record |
| No trust from network location | Sitting inside `10.100.0.0/24` grants nothing — the wristband check runs regardless of source plane |
| Transport is an auth event, not a route | WG terminates at the gateway; interior planes (`10.200`, `10.100`) are unreachable *by* a peer — they are delivered *into*, header-first |

Right — laptop = WG peer, so it's on the far side of the door. It **cannot** hit `10.200.0.1:50052` directly (that's the thing we just established — WG is left at the valet). So the laptop shim must point at the **xray door**, not at the interior gRPC plane. The door is what injects the headers and forwards inward to cognitive-mcp.

Laptop `~/.gemini/settings.json`:

```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "cognitive-mcp": {
      "command": "/usr/local/bin/op-mcp-shim",
      "args": ["--endpoint", "https://<DOOR_ADDR>:<DOOR_PORT>"],
      "trust": true,
      "timeout": 30000
    }
  }
}
```

What changes versus the inside-node version, and why:

- **Endpoint is the xray ingress, reachable from the laptop** — i.e. either the WG-gateway-side address (`10.0.0.1`-ish, over the tunnel) or the public REALITY front, depending on which xray listener fronts cognitive-mcp's gRPC. Not `10.200.x`.
- **`https://`, not `http://`** — the door is the TLS/REALITY termination. The shim's tonic channel needs TLS here (it had it for the grpc-bridge path). If reflection fails on TLS, that's the likely culprit and I'll patch the channel builder.
- **Dropped the `env` footprint/trace headers** — the door stamps `X-Ghostbridge-Footprint`/`X-Ghostbridge-Trace-ID` from your live sled. If the laptop sets them itself they're either ignored or, worse, fail the hologram check because the laptop can't compute tonight's Blake3 footprint. **Let the door do it.** The laptop just needs to get *through* the WG handshake (which stamps the sled); after that the header injection is server-side.

The one thing I need from you to make this exact: **which address+port does xray expose cognitive-mcp's gRPC to a WG peer?** Two ways to find it:

Yes — it's a genuinely good analogy, and specifically good *for LLMs*, for a concrete reason: it maps **one-to-one** onto the mechanism instead of just being decorative. Each metaphor object has exactly one technical referent:

| Analogy | Mechanism | Clean mapping? |
|---|---|---|
| Valet parks the car | WG terminates at gateway | ✅ exact |
| Car never moves again | tunnel not forwarded inward | ✅ exact |
| Claim ticket | session_id (per-visit) | ✅ exact |
| Wristband | identity sled in /dev/shm | ✅ exact |
| Hologram under UV | Blake3 footprint check | ✅ exact |
| Serial number | trace_id → Qdrant | ✅ exact |
| VIP section pass | nextdns_profile | ✅ exact |
| Guard re-checks every door | interceptor on every call | ✅ exact |

That 1:1 property is *why* an LLM absorbs it well — there's no slippage to hallucinate into. A bad analogy is one where the metaphor implies things the system doesn't do (e.g. "fortress with a moat" implies a perimeter — which would actively *mislead* here, since your whole point is there's no perimeter, the wristband is checked everywhere).

Two small places where it's so absorbable it could mislead a model that takes it literally — worth a one-line guardrail each:

1. **"Wristband"** implies a *physical token the bearer carries*. But the bearer never holds the sled — it lives in venue shared memory; the bearer just proved they can read it. An LLM might infer "client sends the wristband" → wrong. Tighten to: *the wristband is painted on the venue wall in your spot; you don't carry it, the guard reads it.*

2. **"Checks the wristband, not your car"** is great, but a model might conclude the car (WG) is therefore *useless after entry*. It's not — it's the thing that proved identity *once*. Frame it as **"the valet interaction IS the ID check"**, not "the car is discarded."

Net: it's the rare analogy that makes the system *more* accurately compressible, not less. Keep it. If anything, lead the Inception narrative with it — §4 lightbulb moment #1 ("preserve origin, hide transport") is the valet line in disguise.

Yes — **"hand stamp" is strictly better than "wristband,"** and it fixes the exact guardrail problem I flagged. Here's why it maps tighter:

| Property of the sled | Wristband | Hand stamp |
|---|---|---|
| You don't carry a separate token | ✗ (a band is an object) | ✅ it's *on you*, nothing to hold or hand over |
| Invisible until checked | ✗ (always visible) | ✅ **only shows under the guard's UV light** |
| Can't be passed to someone else | weak | ✅ it's stamped on *your* skin |
| Re-checked at every door, instantly | ok | ✅ that's literally how clubs use it |
| Tied to tonight only | ok | ✅ washes off / today's ink color |

The killer detail: **the stamp is invisible until UV hits it.** That's exactly your sled — it sits in `/dev/shm` doing nothing visible, and only when the interceptor shines its "UV" (the zero-copy mmap read + Blake3 footprint compare) does the mark fluoresce as valid-or-not. A forged or stale stamp glows wrong under the light. You can't fake the ink because you can't compute tonight's footprint.

And it kills the "client carries the wristband" misread completely — **nobody hands a hand stamp to a bouncer.** The bearer just presents the back of their hand; the venue's own light does the verifying. That's precisely "the caller proved it can read the live sled," not "the caller transmitted a credential."

So the revised line:

> At the door you get an **invisible hand stamp** (the identity sled). You can't see it, you can't take it off and give it to someone, and you can't forge tonight's ink. Every backstage door has a **UV light** (the interceptor) — it shines on your hand, the stamp glows the right color or you're out. The stamp never leaves the venue's own light to verify it.

Want me to swap "wristband" → "hand stamp" throughout the doc and the Inception narrative §4?

**Assistant:**

There's the real cast — these are standard **NIST OSCAL roles**, and they're heavily used (526 `role-id`, 319 `responsible-role`). So the "characters" aren't invented; each is an OSCAL `role-id`, and A.N.N.A. is the one doing **real-time check + approve** — which in OSCAL is precisely the **`content-approver`** (approves the payload) crossed with **`authorizing-official`** (grants authorization to operate). That's literally "checking and approving" in OSCAL vocabulary.

Here's the recovered roster mapped three ways — OSCAL role → concert role → mechanism:

| OSCAL `role-id` | Concert character | What it does in the pipeline |
|---|---|---|
| **`content-approver`** + **`authorizing-official`** | **A.N.N.A. Scribe** — bouncer at every door | **Real-time check + approve every gRPC call**: Blake-light on the hand stamp, footprint match → grant/deny. *This is her.* |
| `creator` / `prepared-by` | the Valet | Gateway notarizes WG arrival, **creates** the sled + Snowball ledger entry |
| `assessor` | the inspector under the light | The footprint *comparison* itself — assesses live schema state vs. the stamp |
| `system-owner` | the venue itself | op-dbus / the schema tree being entered |
| `provider` | transport | WireGuard + xray delivering the peer to the door |
| `operator` / `maintainer` | the crew | s6-supervised services keeping it running |

The key correction locked in: **A.N.N.A. = `authorizing-official`**, and an authorizing-official by definition doesn't sign off once and leave — they hold continuous authority to grant or revoke. That's exactly the "real-time checking and approving" you meant. Her name even encodes it: **N**otary (attests/records → `creator`-ish) + **A**rbitrator (decides/approves → `authorizing-official`).

So she got "lost" because I demoted her to a one-time gateway notary and let an anonymous "interceptor" do the approving. Same person. The interceptor **is** A.N.N.A. acting as authorizing-official on every call.

**Assistant:**

Let me pull the actual salt decision from the code so we're not guessing at what was wired — then I'll weigh first-vs-last octets:

---

**Assistant:**

Pulled the code — and the honest answer is **we never wired an IP-octet salt; it's still the fixed string `WG-STABLE-PSK-2024`** in `wireguard_auth.rs`. The only octet logic that exists (`op-core/src/security.rs:312-328`) is network-*prefix* masking for ACL matching, not salt derivation. So this is a decision to make now, not recover.

Between your two options, **last 2 octets** is the right one — and the reasoning is decisive, not a toss-up:

| | First 2 octets (e.g. `10.100`) | Last 2 octets (e.g. `0.2`) |
|---|---|---|
| What they are | the **network/plane prefix** | the **host portion** (the peer's slot) |
| Across peers | **identical** for everyone on the subnet | **unique per peer** |
| As a salt | ❌ defeats the purpose — a shared salt is no salt | ✅ unique-per-identity, which is exactly a salt's job |

A salt's whole job is to be **unique per identity** so two users with the same input don't derive the same key (and to kill rainbow-table reuse). The first 2 octets are shared by every peer in the plane → useless. The **last 2 octets are the per-peer unique part** → correct.

Two caveats that matter for it to actually work:

1. **Must come from the *stable assigned* IP, not a live/ephemeral one.** You deliberately made the PSK stable so peers don't get locked out mid-session — same rule here. Salt it from `assigned_ip` in `privacy_routes.json` (the permanent `/32` allocation), never from whatever IP the box currently has.

2. **Last-2-octets is only unique within a /16.** `10.100.0.2` and `10.200.0.2` both have last-2 = `0.2` → salt collision across planes. If your peers ever span multiple second-octets, two different users could collide. Cheap fix: salt with the **full `/32`** (all four octets) — still IP-derived, still stable, but globally unique in your space. Same idea you're after, no collision.

So my recommendation: **salt = the host portion of the stable assigned IP, ideally the full `/32`**; if you want literally 2 octets, make it the **last** 2.

One thing to decide so I can log it: do you want the salt to be **just the last 2 octets** (collision-risk across planes, but matches your memory), or the **full assigned `/32`** (same concept, collision-free)? And should I write this into a `SIGNALS`/board note as the resolved key-derivation decision so it stops getting forgotten?

**Assistant:**

You're right, and that kills MAC for this — WireGuard is L3 point-to-point, no Ethernet frames cross the tunnel, so the server **never sees the client's MAC at runtime**. It's only present at provision time (user-typed). Using it as a salt would mean the server stores it and trusts a claim it can't re-observe — exactly the privacy/verifiability problem we're trying to avoid.

So the constraint sharpens: **the salt must be something the server can independently see over WG at runtime.** Over the tunnel, that's basically two things — the **WG pubkey** and the **assigned `/32`**. Both stable, both unique, both mutually observed, and the pubkey is *cryptographically proven* by the handshake (not just claimed).

But there's a trap if you stop there: if the derived key is `Argon2(pubkey, salt=pubkey)`, then everything is a function of the **public** key → anyone who knows the pubkey recomputes the "secret." No actual secret. You need one secret input the server can verify but an attacker can't supply.

That secret is your **PSK** — and here's the nice part: WireGuard has a **built-in preshared-key slot** (`PresharedKey` in the config) that's a symmetric secret mixed into the handshake. Your `--psk` field maps straight onto it. So the clean construction:

```
derived_key = Argon2( secret = PSK , salt = WG_pubkey )
```

- **Salt = WG pubkey** — server-observable over WG, unique, stable, handshake-proven. ✓
- **Secret = PSK** — the real secret; server stores only `blake3(psk)` to verify, never raw. ✓
- **MAC** drops out of the runtime path entirely — keep it (hashed) only as a provision-time hardware binding if you want, but nothing depends on seeing it over the tunnel.

That satisfies everything: only the user holds the real secrets (WG privkey + PSK), the DB stores public pubkey + a derived token + `blake3(psk)`, and the server can verify at runtime using only what it observes over WG.

**2. It becomes stable per-identity, not per-visit.**
The old `BLAKE2s(pubkey + timestamp)` rotated every connection. `session_id = Argon2(PSK, salt=pubkey)` is **deterministic** — same value every session. That's fine *if* you want the session id to be an identity-bound token (it rides inside the already-authenticated WG tunnel + the sled footprint, so replay is bounded). But it's no longer "fresh per visit." If you still want freshness, the only way that stays server-verifiable is a server-issued nonce mixed in at connect time — not the derived key alone.

So the resolved construction becomes:

```
session_id = derived_key = Argon2( secret = PSK , salt = WG_pubkey )

DB stores:  pubkey · UUID v5(pubkey) · blake3(psk) · blake3(session_id)
Runtime:    user presents session_id → server checks blake3(session_id) == stored
            (replay bounded by WG tunnel auth + sled footprint check)
```

**User:**

blkake is always differt, right?

---

**Assistant:**

No — and this is the crux of the whole session-id question, so worth nailing:

**BLAKE (BLAKE2/BLAKE3) is deterministic. Same input → *same* output, every single time.** It's a hash, not a random generator. It looks "always different" only because you fed it something that changes.

That's exactly what was happening in the old design:

```
BLAKE2s(pubkey + timestamp)   → different each connection  — but ONLY because timestamp changes
BLAKE2s(pubkey)               → identical every time        — same input, same hash
Argon2(PSK, salt=pubkey)      → identical every time        — no changing input
```

So the "freshness" never came from BLAKE — it came from the **timestamp** mixed into the input. Remove the timestamp and it's the same value forever.

This decides your session-id question cleanly:

- **If `session_id = Argon2(PSK, pubkey)`** (what "session id = derived key" gives) → it's the **same value every session**. Stable, identity-bound. Not fresh.
- **If you want it to change per visit** → you *must* feed in something that changes: a **timestamp** (predictable, weak) or a **server-issued nonce** (the right answer — unpredictable and server-verifiable).

So the real fork is just: do you want the session id to be **stable** (= the derived key, simplest) or **fresh per visit** (= derived key **mixed with a server nonce** at connect)? The crypto won't make it "automatically different" — that's a choice about whether a changing input goes in.

Which do you want — stable identity-bound, or fresh-per-visit with a nonce?

- `session_id` = stable → proves *who*, survives across sled mutations.

- `mutation_index` + `hashed_footprint` = the sled's content at that datestamp → what the footprint check compares.
- `subid` / `trace_id` = differentiate *what happened* within the current sled.

**Assistant:**

Good question — and it directly affects where I store `blake3(session_id)`, so let me answer it before finishing the code.

## What determines what goes to the Cozo graph

Three gates, in order — and the first is the one you built the taxonomy for:

**1. subid classification (the primary gate).** Gemma classifies every item by subid category (`src/prj/sch/mut/obs/evt/exp`). That classification *is* what decides graph placement and edge type — `mut` (mutation) becomes a state-change node edged to the session, `obs`/`evt` become event nodes, `sch` defines schema nodes, etc. The subid category determines **whether** it's a graph node and **how** it connects. This is exactly why Gemma owns subid + routing.

**2. Persistence + relational nature.** Cozo holds what must *survive reboot* and be *traversed relationally* — durable entities and the edges between them. That's the dividing line against the other two stores:

| Store | Holds | Lifetime | Determined by |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cozo graph** | identity records, soul, domains, feature flags, **relationships** (container→identity→domain→event) | durable | subid says it's a persistent entity/edge |
| **sled** (`/dev/shm`) | current-session footprint, datestamp, mutation_index | ephemeral (current sled only) | runtime, checked per-request |
| **Qdrant** | vectors (semantic) + per-event trace_id records | durable, but vector/log shaped | needs similarity search or is an `evt` trace |

**3. Secret/PII filter.** Cozo stores **public + hashed** only — pubkey, `UUID v5(pubkey)`, `blake3(psk)`, `blake3(session_id)`. Never raw secrets; email only if the client is flagged.

## So the rule in one line
> **Gemma's subid classification decides if an item is a graph node and what it connects to; if it's a durable, relational, non-secret fact → Cozo. Ephemeral runtime state → sled. Vectors/event-traces → Qdrant.**

For the code I'm writing: `blake3(session_id)` is a durable identity fact (subid `src`/identity), non-secret → **Cozo**, written at provision. The live sled just carries the current session_id + footprint for the per-request check.

- Earlier tension: if `session_id = Argon2(PSK, pubkey)` is fully user-derivable, it's not unforgeable; but if the server only stores `blake3(psk)` it can't recompute it.
- **With an official stamp:** the footprint becomes **keyed by the Ghostbridge issuer secret** — e.g. `footprint = HMAC(ghostbridge_issuer_key, session_id ‖ sled_state ‖ datestamp)`. 
  - Only **Ghostbridge can produce** the stamp (it holds the issuer key) → the user **cannot forge** it.
  - Ghostbridge can **verify** it (it has the key) → no need to store/recompute from raw PSK.
  - The stamp is **official** — it carries Ghostbridge's authority, exactly like a rubber stamp carries the venue's seal.

So the layers sharpen:

| Element | Who produces it | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `session_id = Argon2(PSK, pubkey)` | the **user** (from their secrets) | "this is who I am" — the claim |
| **the handstamp / footprint** | **Ghostbridge** (issuer key, at the door) | "officially admitted tonight" — the seal |
| datestamp | the sled | "which night" — currency |
| trace_id | per event | the receipt |

And it adds one key to the storage model we hadn't named: the **Ghostbridge issuer key** — server-side only, the literal rubber stamp. It never goes in Cozo, never in the sled payload, never to the user. It's the thing that makes the footprint *unforgeable*, and it's why A.N.N.A. is the **authorizing-official** — she's the only one who can stamp.

So `derive_session_id` (user side) stays `Argon2(PSK, pubkey)`, but the **footprint/stamp** in the sled becomes an **HMAC under the Ghostbridge issuer key**, not a plain hash. That's the line that changes in the interceptor/sled-write path.

**Assistant:**

Two separate things with two different homes — and the second question has a sharp answer:

## Where the issuer key (rubber stamp) goes
**Not Cozo.** Cozo holds public + hashed identity facts; a private issuer key is the crown jewel. It goes in the **encrypted key store / vault**, same place the WG private keys live — `op-gateway/src/encrypted_storage.rs` (Argon2-encrypted), referenced by handle. Your codebase already has the pattern: `op-blockchain/DESIGN.md` uses `"vault://op-dbus/privacy/hash-salt"`. So:

| Thing | Home | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **Ghostbridge issuer key** (the stamp die) | vault / encrypted key store | private; never leaves the server; Cozo at most holds a `vault://…` **reference**, never the key |
| **the stamp value** (`HMAC(issuer_key, …)`) | sled (runtime) + Cozo record | it's a MAC, safe to store — having it doesn't let you forge new ones |

So the key that *makes* stamps is vaulted; the stamps it *produces* are storable anywhere.

## Can a field be ACL in Cozo?
**Not enforced natively — no.** Cozo is an embedded Datalog/relational engine; it has **no built-in column/row-level access control** (no Postgres-style RLS, no per-field permissions). It won't stop a reader of the DB from seeing a column.

**But** — and this fits your architecture perfectly — Cozo is a *graph* DB, so you **model ACL as data**: relations/edges like `can_read(role, field)`, `grants(stamp, scope)`. Cozo is *good* at representing the ACL graph. The **enforcement** then happens where it already belongs in your system: at the **D-Bus projection / interceptor (A.N.N.A.)** layer — "every mutation is an enforcement point." The mirror decides what projects to whom based on the ACL edges + OSCAL tags; Cozo just *stores* the policy graph, A.N.N.A. *enforces* it.

So the rule:
> **Cozo represents ACL (as a graph); it doesn't enforce it. Enforcement is at the projection/interceptor layer. Secrets never go in Cozo — they go in the vault, referenced by handle.**

That actually keeps your zero-trust principle intact: the DB is dumb storage, the gate is the wristband-check at every door, and the one secret that issues stamps sits in the vault behind A.N.N.A.

Want me to record both (issuer key → vault; Cozo ACL = modeled-not-enforced, enforced at projection) in `SIGNALS.md`, and note it on OD-24?

so there is going to be an identity vault and a user store (db)

**Assistant:**

Exactly — two persistent stores with a hard boundary between them, plus the runtime tiers that derive from them:

## The two stores

**🔐 Identity Vault** — *secrets only.* The crown jewels, encrypted, server-only, never projected, referenced by `vault://` handle.
- Ghostbridge **issuer key** (the rubber-stamp die)
- Server-held WG private material / encryption keys
- Anything that, if leaked, lets you forge or impersonate

**📇 User Store (Cozo DB)** — *public + hashed records and relationships.* Queryable, traversable, projectable (under ACL).
- WG **public** key (canonical identity anchor)
- `UUID v5(pubkey)` MCP token
- `blake3(psk)`, `blake3(session_id)` — verification hashes
- soul / domains / feature flags
- the **ACL graph** (modeled as edges)
- email **only if flagged**

## The boundary rule
> **If having it lets you forge or impersonate → Vault. If it's public, hashed, or relational → User Store.** The User Store may hold *references* into the Vault (`vault://…`), never the secret itself.

## How the runtime tiers relate
The two stores are the **source of truth**; the fast tiers are **derived**:

```
🔐 Identity Vault ─(issuer key)─┐
                                ├─► sled (/dev/shm)  = current session: session_id + datestamped footprint (stamped via issuer key)
📇 User Store (Cozo) ─(records)─┘
                                └─► Qdrant           = vectors (semantic) + trace_id event records
```

- **Vault + User Store** = durable, authoritative.
- **sled** = ephemeral projection of "who's live right now," footprint stamped using the vaulted issuer key.
- **Qdrant** = derived search/accountability layer.

So at provision: secrets land in the **Vault**, records land in the **User Store**. At runtime: A.N.N.A. pulls the issuer key from the Vault to stamp, checks the presented session_id against the User Store hash, etches the sled, logs the trace to Qdrant.

**Assistant:**

No — and the distinction matters, so let me be precise: the **raw `session_id` is stored in *neither*.** Each store holds a *different representation*, and only one of them touches the session at all.

| Store | Holds re: session_id | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **🔐 Vault** | **nothing** | The vault is only for things that let you *forge/issue* — the issuer key, private keys. `session_id` isn't an issuing secret, so it has no business there. |
| **📇 User Store (DB)** | `blake3(session_id)` — the **hash only** | Enough to *verify* a presented session_id (`blake3(presented) == stored`), useless to an attacker who steals the DB. Never the raw value. |
| **sled (`/dev/shm`)** | the **raw** session_id, transiently | Only while the session is live; ephemeral, zero-copy, gone on reboot. |
| **user's machine** | recomputes the raw on demand | `Argon2(PSK, pubkey)` from their own secrets — they don't even need to persist it. |

So the rule holds: **the raw secret-ish value lives user-side + ephemerally in the sled; the DB keeps only its hash; the vault keeps only forging keys.**

Why not put raw `session_id` in the DB or vault? Because it's effectively a **bearer credential** — whoever holds the raw value can present it. Storing it at rest server-side would make a DB leak = account takeover. Storing only `blake3(session_id)` means a leaked DB reveals nothing usable, exactly like storing password hashes instead of passwords.

The one place the raw exists server-side is the **live sled**, and that's deliberate: it's in `tmpfs`, current-session-only, checked at zero-copy speed, and evaporates. That's the "current wristband on the wall," not a stored record.

So: **DB gets the hash, vault gets nothing, sled holds the live raw, user recomputes it.** Want me to lock this (session_id: hash-in-DB, raw-in-sled-only, never-vaulted) into the spec?

**Assistant:**

Now we're at the real crux, and your instinct splits exactly right along the two client tiers:

## The linkage only exists for clients that opt in
- **Privacy / GhostBridge clients:** the **key *is* the identity.** No person behind it, no email, no user↔key link. The pubkey stands alone — there's nothing to link, by design. No dilemma.
- **Corporate / compliance clients:** they **choose** to bind a real user to the key — because accountability/audit/non-repudiation is the thing they're buying. *They* create the dilemma by opting into it.

So "how do you link the user with the key" is **only a compliance-tier question.** Privacy clients never have it.

## What the join key is, and where the link lives
The **pubkey is the join** across stores — it's the one stable, public anchor present everywhere. Then for compliance clients only:

| Store | Holds (compliance client) | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **📇 User Store (DB/Cozo)** | edge: `pubkey → real_user` (PII), `pubkey → blake3(session_id)` | operational lookup + audit graph |
| **🔐 Vault** | the **sealed de-anon binding** — the user↔key link **encrypted**, openable only under authorization | lawful/compliance recovery, gated by A.N.N.A. |

This is where your "**in both**" instinct is correct — but it's not the *raw derived key* duplicated, it's two **different roles** of the binding:
- **DB** holds the *operational, queryable* link (hashes + the pubkey↔user edge) — used every request.
- **Vault** holds the *sealed escrow* of the de-anonymizing binding — **encrypted, opened only under authorization** (A.N.N.A. as authorizing-official, an auditor's warrant, a compliance officer's grant). A DB leak alone can't de-anonymize; you also need the vault to unseal.

That's exactly the compliance property corporates pay for: **the identity↔person link exists and is recoverable, but only under authority** — not casually readable, not leaked by a DB dump. Privacy clients simply have **no vault binding at all**, so there's nothing to unseal — they're cryptographically unlinkable because the link was never created.

So:
> **Privacy client:** key alone, no link, no escrow.
> **Compliance client:** pubkey↔user edge in the DB (operational) + sealed binding in the Vault (recoverable only under A.N.N.A. authorization). The "in both" = operational-hash in DB, sealed-escrow in Vault — same binding, two roles.

That keeps the raw secret-never-stored rule intact while giving compliance clients true, authority-gated non-repudiation.

Want me to record this — **two-tier clients; compliance-only user↔key link; DB = operational edge, Vault = sealed authority-gated escrow** — into the spec, and flag the de-anon path as an A.N.N.A.-authorized operation on OD-24?
